# m0irai — FINDINGS (the improvements ledger)

**What this is.** One row per thing FOUND during the extraction — a defect, a harness blind spot, an unexplained event, a design gap, an M2 candidate — with its evidence and what was decided. `docs/STATE.md` records what was *proved*, in order; this file records what was *found*, across phases, so nothing found is lost when a phase closes. Deviations from the plan (STATE's D-items) are decisions, not findings, and stay in STATE.

**Rule (operator, 2026-08-18).** A row is added — or its status flipped — **in the same commit as the work that finds or fixes it**. Backfilled rows cite the record they were taken from. Rows are never deleted; a row that turns out to be wrong is struck through with the reason. The "Ledger history" at the end names every commit that touched this file.

**Columns.** `id` stable, never reused · `found` date and by whom (lead = the extraction lead; `p5 lane` = the Phase 5 builder; `rust lane` = the Phase 4 builder; `codex #n` = the cross-family audit of 2026-08-18, `.lead/reviews/codex-phase5-review.md`) · `area` · `sev` — **BLOCK** would stop the product or a proof · **MAJOR** a defect with a real failure path · **MINOR** hygiene / wording / tooling · **INFO** a measurement or decision worth keeping · `status` — `FIXED @<commit>` (lane hashes are cited for work merged by squash; the merge commit itself is never cited by the file it contains) · `DESIGN` resolved by design, reason given · `REFUTED` investigated, not a defect · `OPEN` recorded, no home yet · `M2` deliberately deferred to milestone 2 · `→3.8` / `→Phase 5c` / `→Phase 6` / `→Phase 7` deferred to a named M1 step · `DECIDED` a product/engineering decision on record · `UNEXPLAINED` observed, cause not established.

**Local names.** Phases numbered their findings independently (Phase 0 F1–F4, Phase 3 F5–F8, Phase 4 F9, the Rust lane's F1–F5, the Phase 5 lane's F1–F6, codex #1–#20, K1–K3). Each row keeps its local name in bold so STATE and the lane records still cross-reference; the ledger id is the stable one.

### Cross-family audit (codex gpt-5.6-sol, effort ultra, read-only, 2,310 s — 2026-08-18)
Every item was verified against the code before anything was built; #2 and #3 are FL-035 and FL-041 above.

| id | found | area | sev | status | finding | evidence · fix · decision |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FL-044 | 2026-08-18 codex #1 | Job Object (packaged product) | BLOCK | FIXED (Node half lane `b3716c1` @ Phase 5; Rust half = this commit, lane `426247c..835b3bd`) — R4(b) green, R4(c) = the permanent falsifier | **F10** — `detached: true` does not escape the Rust binary's `JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE` job (`rust/crates/codegen/xai-tty-utils/src/lib.rs:485-507`; `host_process.rs` `scope.enroll`): libuv never sets `CREATE_BREAKAWAY_FROM_JOB`, so the close digest the host forks dies the instant the Rust process exits — in the packaged product only; every dev/test path runs with no job, which is why nothing red pointed at it | probe `.lead/tools/job-survival-probe.ps1`: KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE **KILLED** · +BREAKAWAY_OK **KILLED** · +SILENT_BREAKAWAY_OK SURVIVED (rejected — every host child, agent CLIs and PTYs included, would escape the reaper) · no job SURVIVED. Fix = Phase 5c: with `ZER0_DIGEST_HANDOFF=1` (set by the Rust launcher) the host writes the close digest as a durable request (`<repoRoot>/.zer0/journal/digest-requests.jsonl`) instead of spawning; the Rust binary consumes it after the host exits and spawns the identical child outside the job; Rust survival test + KILLED falsifier. |

| FL-063 | 2026-08-18 5c Rust lane | brief (lead premise) | MINOR | FIXED in the lane (`e1af5d2`) | the lead's 5c brief claimed the falsifier R4(c) "already reproduces F10 against the current host"; measured at `d8dae89`: the host forks NO close digest at all (`spawnDetachedDigest`/`bootCatchUp` have no caller outside `src/memory/`; no `room-close` anywhere) — R4(c) would have been a green tautology | the lane built `a_handed_off_child_outlives_the_job_that_kills_a_child_the_host_forked` instead: a mock host forks a CONTROL child (marker at once) and a SUBJECT child (marker after 3 s) with `digestSpawnArgs()`'s exact options, plus one handed-off request; after the job closes: control written, handed-off written, subject absent = F10 reproduced and fixed with no Node dependency. THE IDLE-ROOM SINGLE-PRESS EXIT IS INTENDED BEHAVIOUR, NOT A DEFECT.** So if the room was idle when they held Ctrl+C, the app closing was CORRECT and nothing here is broken. ⚠ **THE OTHER HALF IS STILL A REAL HOLE AND IS WORTH FIXING ON ITS OWN EVIDENCE, INDEPENDENT OF WHICH PATH THEY HIT:** on a BUSY room the quiet-valve/quit-arm mismatch below lets a held key quit without a release, and that is a defect whether or not it is the one the operator met. **Which of the two they actually hit is still unknown and the flight recorder settles it** — that is why the recorder slice records room state per key rather than keys alone. ⚠ **The lead asked the operator which state the room was in and did NOT get that answer; they answered the product question instead. Not re-asked — the guard fix does not depend on it.** ⚠⚠ **CAUSE FOUND 2026-08-21 BY A CROSS-FAMILY CODEX REVIEW, AND IT WAS A DESIGN TRADE THE FIX MADE ON PURPOSE AND A TEST DOCUMENTED AS A FEATURE.** `CTRL_C_GESTURE_QUIET` is **1 second** (`room_ctrl_c_gesture.rs:65`) and `key_down` starts a FRESH gesture whenever the gap since the last Ctrl+C is `>= ` that (`:86-94`), while the quit arm stays live for **3 seconds**. So: press #1 cancels and arms quit → the autorepeat stream goes quiet for a second → the NEXT autorepeat `Press` counts as a brand-new first tap → **the still-live quit arm fires and the app closes, with the key never released.** ⚠ **The lane wrote a test that PROVES this exit and framed it as the escape hatch** — `a_quiet_gesture_can_still_quit_without_any_release` (`room_runtime_cancel_tests.rs:1824`), whose own comment reads *"The valve has to open INSIDE the confirmation window or it opens onto a room that has already disarmed"*. **The valve opening inside the arm IS the defect and the test is its proof, read the other way up.** The ConPTY held-key test never catches it: three key-downs back-to-back inside 600 ms, so the >=1s gap is never exercised. ⚠ **BUT THE LEAD MEASURED THIS MACHINE AND THE PATH DOES NOT FIT IT AS STATED:** `HKCUControl PanelKeyboard` reports `KeyboardDelay=1` (~500 ms initial delay) and `KeyboardSpeed=31` (fastest, ~33 ms), so a held key's first repeat lands at ~500 ms — INSIDE the quiet window, not past it. **Codex said so itself rather than overclaiming: *"I cannot prove which path closed the operator's app."*** ⚠ **THE ROUTE THAT ACTUALLY FITS, AND IT NEEDS ONE ANSWER FROM THE OPERATOR:** codex enumerated the state-dependent paths and found *"idle room with empty composer: first Ctrl+C exits"* and *"open picker: first Ctrl+C exits"* (`room_runtime.rs:1019`) — **on an IDLE room a single press closes the app, held or not, and the gesture guard is not involved at all.** Every other operator report this wave was about cancelling a RUNNING turn. **If the room was idle when they held Ctrl+C, nothing about the held-key guard is implicated and the real finding is that an idle room quits on one unconfirmed keypress.** THE FLIGHT RECORDER SLICE ALREADY IN FLIGHT SETTLES THIS, because it records room state alongside each key. ⚠ **UPDATED 2026-08-21 09:2x AFTER A GROK-FIRST SWEEP THAT THE OPERATOR ORDERED. Four facts were MEASURED, and two of them retire hypotheses the lead was carrying.** **(a) crossterm NEVER emits `KeyEventKind::Repeat` on Windows.** The kind is a binary function of one field: `let kind = if key_event.key_down { Press } else { Release }` (`crossterm-0.28.1/src/event/sys/windows/parse.rs:286-290`, and identically at `:223-227`). **Slice B's header claim was RIGHT**, and it follows that upstream's own repeat guard — *"Never let an auto-repeat (held key) drive the destructive Ctrl+X arm→confirm"* (`D:/grok-ref/.../views/dashboard/state.rs:3521-3527`) — **is dead code on Windows.** Adopting grok's mechanism here would have shipped the same bug with a citation attached. **(b) crossterm DOES deliver `Release` on Windows.** No filter exists in `handle_key_event` (`parse.rs:42-61`) or the event source (`src/event/source/windows.rs:43-70`); the comment at `parse.rs:209` claiming *"We normally ignore all key release events"* **contradicts the code six lines below it**. **(c) `enable_raw_mode` clears `ENABLE_PROCESSED_INPUT`** (`crossterm-0.28.1/src/terminal/sys/windows.rs:18,36`), so Ctrl+C arrives as a key event, not a `CTRL_C_EVENT` signal. ⚠ **CORRECTION — (d) AS WRITTEN IS FALSE, AND THE LEAD'S OWN INSTRUMENT CAUSED IT.** `rust/.../app/signal_handler.rs:112` DOES call `tokio::signal::ctrl_c()` in production code and `app/mod.rs:1410` installs it. **The conclusion survives and the claim does not:** `lib.rs:5` gates that module behind the `grok-runtime` feature and `zer0-v2-bin/Cargo.toml:11` enables only `room-runtime`, so **it is never compiled into the shipped room.** ⚠ **HOW THE LEAD GOT IT WRONG: the grep was capped with `head -20` and the twenty lines returned were all test helpers, so the real hit was cut off the bottom and the truncation was read as an absence.** That is the absent-signal law, self-inflicted by a cap the lead added, on the same day the lead recorded FL-131 about believing an empty result. **A cap on a search whose emptiness you intend to believe is the same defect as `2>/dev/null`.** ⚠ **ALSO NARROWED — (a) is right in its conclusion and too broad as written:** `Repeat` is indeed never constructed on Windows, but the kind is not universally a function of `key_down` — the surrogate-pair branch calls `KeyEvent::new`, which always sets `Press` (`crossterm-0.28.1/src/event.rs:757`). No effect on Ctrl+C. **ORIGINAL, PARTLY WRONG:** **(d) no production Ctrl+C signal handler exists in our tree** (positive control: `signal_handler.rs` is present but every `ctrl_c` grep hit is a test helper). ⚠ **TOGETHER THESE KILL THE LEAD'S OWN LEADING HYPOTHESIS** — *"if Windows Terminal reports no key-up, the gesture has nothing to close on"*. **It reports key-ups. The mechanism is NOT starved of its input, so the cause is somewhere else and is still unknown.** ⚠ **AND THE MEASUREMENT INSTRUMENT ALREADY EXISTS — WE DO NOT HAVE TO BUILD ONE.** Upstream ships an input flight recorder and **the file is already in our tree**: *"Input flight recorder — rolling buffer of recent key events. Ctrl+Shift+D dumps to `~/.grok/logs/input-debug-<timestamp>.json`"* (`rust/crates/codegen/xai-grok-pager/src/input_log.rs:1-3`), recording `kind: "Press", "Repeat", "Release"` per event (`:75`). ⚠ **It is NOT wired into the room** — `input_log` appears in seven files including `app/agent_view/mod.rs` and `views/prompt_widget/mod.rs`, and in **neither** `room_view.rs` nor `app/room_runtime.rs` (positive control run). **REVISED NEXT ACTION: wire the existing recorder into the room's key intake and ship it, then the operator holds Ctrl+C, presses Ctrl+Shift+D, and hands over the JSON. Fix against that file, not against a theory.** ⚠ **One correction to this row's own earlier text: slice B DID read upstream first** — its module header cites four grok `file:line` refs and states why it deviated (`room_ctrl_c_gesture.rs:37-52`). The failure was not skipped research. It was **a correct reading of upstream, a correct deviation, and a wrong conclusion about which signal was missing** — which is exactly the failure a flight recorder ends and no amount of further reasoning will. **CAUSE UNKNOWN — do not guess, reproduce.** ⚠ **The strongest lead, and it is only a lead:** the cross-family review prescribed tracking the gesture *through RELEASE where the terminal reports it*, and **failing safe on terminals that have no release semantics**. If Windows Terminal reports no key-up for Ctrl+C, then the whole gesture model has nothing to close on, and whatever the fail-safe branch does is what the operator is actually hitting. **The next step is a measurement, not a patch: capture the raw key events Windows Terminal emits for a held Ctrl+C on this machine, and read what actually arrives.** ⚠ **WHAT THIS COSTS US, stated plainly:** slice B added 27 tagged proofs, 21 mutations, a real-ConPTY test, and a fix lane added 427 more lines of tests — **and every one of them passes while the defect is live.** The ConPTY harness was even extended to emit key-UPS specifically so a held key could be distinguished, which means **the harness now models a terminal behaviour the operator's real terminal does not have.** That is a test that agrees with itself. ⚠ **FIFTH instance of this wave's dominant class** (FL-100, FL-117, FL-118, FL-125): *the interface asserting something the system has not done* — here the assertion is by the TEST SUITE about the product. **Per this project's own rule, a defect class seen this many times becomes a GATE, not another patch.** THE GATE THIS WANTS: nothing may claim a real-terminal key behaviour without a capture from the operator's actual terminal. |

it is that the codebase classifies cancellation at four independent places and nothing enumerates them.** The honest fix is probably not a fifth patch: it is to make the signal reachable at ONE place every terminal outcome must pass through, so a new exit cannot be added without confronting it. ⚠ **AND ONE MORE, LEFT EXPLICITLY UNVERIFIED BY THE REVIEWER RATHER THAN GUESSED AT:** an ACP result that wins the race and returns **accepted** just before or after the abort bypasses the signal check entirely. **Upstream checks local cancellation even on a SUCCESSFUL result** (`D:/grok-ref/.../app/dispatch/prompt.rs:1192-1195,1345-1365`) — we do not. **That may be the same defect the operator is seeing in FL-139, where claude and codex answered through an Esc cancel.** Do not treat that as established; it is the strongest open lead. |

| FL-146 | 2026-08-21 **13-agent adversarial workflow, measured from the operator's own evidence DB; every number below re-run by the lead against `D:/m0irai-playground/.zer0/evidence.db` before being written here** | cancellation / the room dispatches AFTER a cancel | **BLOCK — the most serious defect on the board** | OPEN — **root cause established at file:line, fix seam identified, not built** | ⚠⚠ **CORRECTED 2026-08-21 12:51 BY THE OPERATOR'S OWN REFUTATION. THE MECHANISM BELOW IS RIGHT; THE TRIGGER THE LEAD NAMED — 'COLD vs WARM BRIDGE' — IS WRONG.** The lead wrote that a cold bridge causes the failure and that it predicted all five trials *"with no exceptions"*. **The operator then ran EIGHT MORE TRIALS AND ALL EIGHT CANCELLED CORRECTLY, INCLUDING THREE ON COLD BRIDGES.** Measured: at `09:49:38.614` they submit, `09:49:39.767` gemini is cancelled, **codex's prompt is sent at `09:49:43.201` — 3.4 s AFTER the cancel, exactly like the failures — and codex is cancelled anyway at `09:49:48.047`.** Same at `09:50:13.145` (3.0 s late, cancelled) and `09:50:15.753` (5.6 s late, cancelled). **A cold bridge is NOT sufficient to cause the defect, so the lead's stated predictor was false and is retracted.** ⚠ **Their words, which are the correct description: *"sometimes it works and sometimes it doesn't"* — that is a RACE, and neither the key nor the bridge temperature decides it.** ✅ **THE REAL WINDOW, read out of `src/chat/lane-hold.ts` after the refutation:** `dropHold` (`:134-138`) calls `replaceHold`, which touches **only `state.held`** and returns immediately when nothing is held (`:186-188`). A connection being opened lives in `state.opening` (`:157`) and is superseded only by the epoch check `if (epoch > state.closedThrough) return conn;` (`:158`) — **and `closedThrough` is bumped in exactly one place, `closeHoldState` (`:209`), which `dropHold` never calls.** **So the race is the gap between the connection existing and `installHold` moving it into `state.held` (`:193-194`):** cancel lands after the install and `dropHold` kills it — **it works**; cancel lands while the connection is still merely *opening* and `dropHold` is a **complete no-op that returns `false`** — the open finishes, the hold installs, and the carrier sends the prompt to an agent the operator already stopped. **That single window explains all eleven trials — the three failures, the five earlier successes, AND the eight the operator used to refute the first account.** ⚠ **THE LEAD'S PROCESS ERROR, recorded because it is the reusable part: eleven data points fitted the cold/warm story and the story was still wrong.** Five successes and three failures were consistent with it, and the lead called it *proven* rather than *not yet contradicted* — **then the operator produced the contradicting case in five minutes by simply running it more times.** **A predictor that has never been given a chance to fail has not been tested.** **ORIGINAL, PARTLY WRONG ACCOUNT:** ⚠⚠ **THE ROOM ACCEPTS A CANCEL, STOPS ONE AGENT, AND THEN SENDS THE PROMPT TO THE OTHER TWO ANYWAY.** Not a cancel that failed — a cancel that was **obeyed for gemini and then followed by fresh dispatches to claude and codex.** **MEASURED, from the operator's 11:36 trial, re-queried by the lead:** `08:36:47.120 user submits` → `08:36:48.122 gemini cancelled` → **`08:36:51.875 claude's prompt SENT` (3.7 s AFTER the cancel)** → `08:36:56.803 claude completed` → **`08:36:55.640 codex's prompt SENT` (7.5 s AFTER the cancel)** → `08:37:07.594 codex completed`. Both marked `resolved = accepted` in `lane_prompt_attempts`. ⚠ **The discriminator is COLD vs WARM bridge, and it predicts all five of the operator's trials with no exceptions:** when both prompts leave within ~2 ms of each other the bridge was warm and both die in flight (`resolved = null`); when they leave seconds apart the bridge was cold-booting and the prompt goes out after the cancel. ⚠ **THE ALTERNATION IS SELF-INFLICTED: a cancel that WORKS kills the bridge, so the NEXT turn must cold-boot — which is precisely the state where the next cancel is a no-op.** Cold fails, warm works, and a warm cancel makes the next one cold. | **ROOT CAUSE, at file:line.** `dropHold` releases a connection that is already HELD and does nothing about one that is being OPENED: `src/chat/lane-hold.ts:134-138`, and `replaceHold` returns immediately when `prior === undefined` (`:186-188`). **`state.opening` (`:157`) and the supersession counter `state.closedThrough` are touched ONLY by `closeHoldState` (`:208-216`) and never by `dropHold`.** So the in-flight `openFresh` survives its epoch check (`:160`), `installHold` accepts it (`:195-205`), and the carrier sends the prompt. ✅ **The fix seam already exists in the same file — `closeHoldState` does the right thing (bump the epoch, drain `opening`); `dropHold` simply does not call it.** ⚠ **AND THE DEEPER FACT, which explains why FOUR seams have now been found: THE ACP CARRIER IS SIGNAL-BLIND.** `CarrierTurnInput` (`src/chat/lane-carrier.ts:122-151`) **has no `signal` field at all** — the only abort bound to an ACP bridge child (`acp-lane-connection.ts:333-343`) comes from `room-host.ts:137`, aborted in exactly one place: `room-host.ts:285`, inside `shutdown()`. **That is a process-shutdown signal, not the turn's.** So `active.controller.abort()` (`room-engine.ts:486`) reaches NOTHING on an ACP lane, and the lane's entire per-turn stop is the process kill that the cold path skips. **Gemini is unaffected because it holds the turn's own signal** (`headless-carrier.ts:309` → `agy-runner.ts:113-119`). ⚠ **WHY NOBODY SAW IT: `void this.options.onCancel?.(target)` is unawaited with its rejection unhandled (`room-engine.ts:487`), and `dropLaneHold`'s boolean return is DISCARDED (`lane-transport.ts:246-249`) — so a cancel that killed nothing is indistinguishable from one that worked.** Seventh instance of the wave's dominant class, and the one that caused the operator's original report. |
| FL-147 | 2026-08-21 **same workflow, found while ruling the key out as the cause** | cancellation / Esc swallowed by the permission shelf | MAJOR | OPEN — **real, key-asymmetric, and NOT the cause of FL-139** | ⚠ **AN ACTIVE ACP PERMISSION SHELF SWALLOWS ESC AND SENDS NO CANCEL AT ALL.** `room_runtime.rs:1346-1349` — `if key.code == KeyCode::Esc { room.permissions.focus_composer(); return PermissionKeyRoute::Consumed; }` — **no modifier check, and `focus_composer` only clears `focused`, so the shelf never leaves `is_active()`** (`room_permission_view.rs:170-172,192-196`). Ctrl+C matches none of that function's arms and falls through to the cancel. ⚠ **Permission asks are ACP-ONLY** (`src/adapters/acp/acp-permission.ts`; **zero** permission references under `src/adapters/pty/`, positive control: `agy` matches nine files there) — **so this hole is claude-and-codex-only and gemini-immune, which is the exact shape of the operator's report.** It is a genuine second explanation of the same symptom | ⚠ **REPORTED AS NOT-THE-CAUSE RATHER THAN AS A SECOND WIN, because the evidence says so:** FL-146's cold-bridge timing predicts all five of the operator's trials from the recorded data, and this one would require a permission shelf to have been open, which the run does not show. **A finding that fits the symptom is not the same as a finding that fits the data, and the difference is the whole discipline.** ⚠ **It will still bite:** Esc has **five** consumers ahead of its cancel — the permission shelf (`:1346`), the composer menu (`:884-890`), the slash menu (`:913-916`), the file-search gate (`:936`), and the picker — while Ctrl+C's ladder sits at `:828-836`. **Esc is nearly the LAST consumer of its own keystroke and Ctrl+C is nearly the first**, so the two keys are only equivalent in an empty room. **The operator believes they are the same button.** |
| FL-148 | 2026-08-21 **the `fl146-cancel-race` lane, reported unprompted while working around it** | gates / a clamp that stops counting | **BLOCK** | OPEN — **the lane worked around it rather than changing the gate; nothing is fixed** | ⚠⚠ **`gate-clamps` COUNTS RAW BRACES, SO ONE UNBALANCED `{` INSIDE A STRING LITERAL SWITCHES OFF THE FUNCTION-LENGTH GATE FOR THE REST OF THE FILE.** `countFunctionLines` runs to EOF once the brace depth never returns to zero. **A live instance is in the tree:** `src/room/room-host-support.test.ts:57` contains a template literal of the shape `${first}{"bad":true` with no closing brace, **so that file's function-length clamp is silently dead from line 57 to the end — and so is every file with that shape.** The gate keeps printing a pass | ⚠ **EIGHTH INSTANCE OF THE WAVE'S DOMINANT CLASS AND THE FIFTH INSTRUMENT TO BE CAUGHT DOING NOTHING TODAY** — after FL-131 (a gate passing because it could not see the file), FL-138 (an agent citing a control path that did not exist), FL-142 (the lead's own table check matching zero rows for a whole day), and the lead's `git diff` snapshot at shutdown that reported 361 bytes for a lane holding 34,708 because it could not see the index. **Five different tools, one failure: a silent pass is indistinguishable from a silent skip.** ⚠ **THE GATE THIS WANTS, and it is the same one FL-142 asked for, now overdue twice: no check may report a pass without also reporting how much it examined.** For this one concretely — `gate-clamps` should report the number of functions it measured per file, and a file whose count is implausibly low against its line count is the signal. **A brace counter that cannot lex a string literal is not a parser and the project's own routing rule says to reach for a real one rather than hand-roll it.** ⚠ **Reported by a builder that had already finished its own slice and was under a shutdown deadline** — it worked around the gate (moved its tests to `room-host-cancel-drop.test.ts`) and **said so instead of letting the workaround pass silently**, which is the only reason this is known. |
| FL-149 | 2026-08-21 22:1x **lead, against the lead — found because T8's runs 5-10 printed nothing and the lead went and read one raw** | lead procedure / a scratch worktree that ate the real tree | **BLOCK** | FIXED by `npm ci` the same hour; **the procedure that caused it is the finding** | ⚠⚠ **THE LEAD DESTROYED THE REPOSITORY'S `node_modules` WITH A `git worktree remove --force`.** To bisect FL-143 cheaply, the lead made a scratch worktree at `D:/m0irai-bisect` and, to avoid a 60 s `npm ci`, created a Windows **junction** `D:/m0irai-bisect/node_modules -> D:/m0irai-work/node_modules`. When the bisect was done, `git worktree remove --force D:/m0irai-bisect` **followed the junction and deleted the contents of the REAL `node_modules`** — `ls node_modules` returned **0 entries**, `better-sqlite3`'s native build and `zod` were gone. **Every `npm run build` on this machine was broken from that moment**, which is why the test's own in-test build started failing with `Cannot find module 'zod'` and the T8 loop silently counted those as shutdown failures | ⚠ **THIS IS THE PARALLEL-WORK LAW'S FIRST LINE, VIOLATED BY THE LEAD:** *one worker, one isolated instance of every shared mutable resource — never a shortcut that shares one.* A junction to the live tree's dependencies is exactly the shared mutable resource the rule forbids, and the rule exists because **the failure mode is silent and lands somewhere else**: the symptom showed up as a different test failing for a different reason, and the lead's own counter dutifully tallied it as more evidence for the wrong bug. ⚠ **TWO THINGS SAVED IT FROM BEING WORSE:** (1) the lead noticed T8's runs 5-10 printed **no probe line at all** — an absent signal — and read one raw instead of accepting the count (the CHECK-BEFORE-ASSERT law, applied for once before the claim rather than after); (2) `npm ci` with the pinned patches is the project's own documented setup path, so recovery is deterministic. ⚠ **THE RULE THAT FALLS OUT, for the resume note: a scratch worktree gets its OWN `npm ci` or it gets nothing. Never link, never junction, never symlink the live tree's `node_modules` — and never `--force` a worktree removal without `ls -la` on what is inside it first.** |

| FL-178 | 2026-08-21 15:5x **the lead, "Evidence DB note" aside during the day2 operator-test triage** (RESUME:104) | memory / lane_prompt_attempts resolution gap | MINOR | OPEN — traced further than the source's own raw note | `lane_prompt_attempts` rows for codex were observed with `resolved=null` after a turn had already failed and been cancelled, which the lead flagged with a question ("should be abandoned?") rather than a diagnosis. | **Traced the full write/resolve graph at `9f03907` to answer the question the source left open.** `resolved` has exactly two non-null values in the schema (`"accepted" \| "abandoned" \| null`, `src/memory/lane-attempts.ts:24`) and exactly two functions that set them: `resolveAttemptAccepted` (`:164`, fires only when the agent's reply is accepted — its one production caller is `runAcceptCommit` in `src/memory/lane-state.ts:249-278`) and `abandonPriorGenerationAttempts` (`:135-151`, fires only on a `generation` bump, superseding all prior-generation unresolved rows at once). **There is no third path.** A prompt attempt that simply fails or is cancelled on the SAME generation and session — no bump, no acceptance — has no function anywhere in the tree that ever touches its `resolved` column again; it stays `NULL` forever, indistinguishable from a genuinely in-flight attempt. | FL-198 | 2026-09-07 **BOOT round-2 review, BLOCKING R2-1** (`lanes-0901/boot-review-r2.md:47-125`) | Rust launcher / Node interpreter selection, refusal fallback resolves to the refused file on Windows | BLOCK | OPEN — fix round 3 running on `boot-fix-r3` (unmerged) | The BOOT lane's own new version check refuses a bad sibling `node.exe` and falls back to the bare name `node.exe` — but Windows resolves an unqualified program name against the **application directory before PATH**, which is exactly where the refused file lives. So the launcher detects the bad interpreter, declines it, and runs it anyway. Proven on the shipped `release-dist` binary: staged with an 18-byte stub named `node.exe` beside `m0irai.exe`, boot dies with `exit=4`, "This version of %1 is not compatible..."; with the stub removed, the same binary boots. The error message's bare `node.exe` (not an absolute path) proves the `Rejected`/`Absent` branch ran, not `Sibling`. Two more reproduced shapes: a zero-byte `node.exe` (refused, then executed, same crash) and a `node.exe` that is actually a directory (never even reaches the version check — `sibling.is_file()` is false, `Absent`, same crash — this second shape is round-1 code, present since base). Consequence: a corrupt, truncated, wrong-architecture or antivirus-mangled `node.exe` in the shipped artifact — realistic ways this file goes bad — produces a dead boot the code, the comment, the test and the round-2 handback all state is survivable. The unit test (`a_sibling_that_cannot_answer_its_version_is_refused_for_a_stated_reason`) only compares an `OsString` to a literal and never spawns a process, so it cannot observe that the literal does not mean PATH here. Unix is unaffected (`execvp` searches PATH only); the product ships Windows. | **Verified unfixed at `40e30d5`.** `choose_node` (`rust/crates/zer0-v2-bin/src/host_process.rs`, reviewer's cited range `:1341-1358`) still returns the bare `NODE_PROGRAM_NAME` on `Rejected`. `git worktree list` confirms `D:/m0irai-work-boot` at `40e30d5` on branch `boot-fix-r3`, live and unmerged. The reviewer's own framing: "the MECHANISM IS INHERITED — falling back to an unqualified `node.exe` has always resolved against the application directory first — and the FALSE GUARANTEE IS ROUND 2's," since round 2 added a refusal whose only escape route leads back to the file it is refusing. Fix belongs at the fallback in `choose_node`, not at the probe. |

| FL-207 | 2026-09-07 **L2 review round 1, BLOCKING B1** (`lanes-0901/l2-review-r1.md:15-54`, at lane tip `7205c1b`); ⚠ **status corrected 2026-09-08 (ledger final round, item D): L2 merged to mainline as `a14e95e`** | shared / process-tree kill-verification, an unref'd poll timer can leave a promise permanently unsettled | BLOCK | **FIXED @ `a14e95e`** (L2, squash of `l2-drain-receipt` rounds 1-5) | ~~`waitForDrain`'s poll `sleep()` uses an `unref()`ed `setTimeout` (`src/shared/kill-tree.ts:401`, at lane tip `7205c1b`; the file is 116 lines at mainline `40e30d5`)~~ (historical, before the fix) — an unref'd timer does not hold the Node event loop open, so if the poll is the only pending work, the process can exit with the promise **unsettled**: no receipt, no error, no return. It fires only when at least one pid survives the initial kill — the exact case the receipt exists to report. Reproduced against the real compiled module (repo untouched): with the unref present, a top-level await never settles (exit 13, "Detected unsettled top-level await"); with one other ref'd timer alive, or with the single `unref()` removed in a scratch copy, it resolves normally. Honest limit stated by the reviewer: not confirmed to fire inside the actual running room host (`shutdown()` is entered via RPC, so stdin is probably still a ref'd handle) — graded BLOCKING anyway because the lane's entire contract is "a seat is stopped only on a receipt," this path returns none, and vitest holds the loop open so no test in the suite can structurally catch it. | **Verified fixed at `a14e95e`.** `src/shared/kill-tree.ts:428-431`'s own comment names the fix directly: "The poll timer is REF'd on purpose. It was unref'd once, and a drain with a surviving pid then never settled at all: the poll was the only pending work, so Node exited 13 with the promise hanging and no receipt returned — in precisely the case the receipt exists to report (review B1, reproduced at exit 13)." The `setTimeout` call itself is `kill-tree.ts:434`, no longer followed by `.unref()`. L2's own round-2 review independently reproduced this with a real child process and confirmed the mutation-tested fix (`l2-review-r2.md:165-189`: RED at exit 13 with `unref()` restored, GREEN as committed). |

Neither holds: `which` 8.0.0's `new_paths` joins the search stem onto each PATH entry as given, without making it absolute, so a relative PATH entry yields a relative candidate that `Command::new` then resolves against the process working directory. Not BLOCKING because it needs a local PATH misconfiguration and the shipped artifact carries a good sibling `node.exe` that never reaches this search — reportable because the round adds a written, checkable invariant that is false, and the absolute-path assertion exists only inside one test (`host_process.rs:1735-1739`) that only fires when the box's PATH happens to yield an absolute candidate. | **Verified at `a14e95e` (BOOT r3 merged as `1c27abe`, ancestor of `a14e95e`).** `host_process.rs:1343` carries the doc comment verbatim; `resolve_node_on_path` is at `:1396`, its `which_all_global` comment at `:1393-1395`, all re-read directly. This is not a regression from B3 — the two guarded assertions were genuinely impossible on POSIX before the fix too — but it is a coverage gap the fix creates by removing the only tests that touched the real kernel path on that platform, worth recording before L3 wires a production caller that will run on both platforms. | **Verified live at `26e47f0`** (mainline moved from `a14e95e` by a config-only commit touching `.gitignore`/`biome.json`; no source file changed, so this citation holds at both hashes). `git show 26e47f0:src/shared/kill-tree.test.ts \| sed -n '25p;185p;204p;223p;244p;257p;273p'`, run this pass: `:25` defines `const WIN32_ONLY = process.platform === "win32";`, and all six `startFixtureTree`-based tests are gated behind `it.runIf(WIN32_ONLY)(` (`:185,204,223,244,257,273`) — confirmed none of the six carries any other guard or platform branch that would let it run on Linux, so both the reviewer's stated consequence and the builder's own skip count follow from the code. `l2-fix-r5.md:113-117` re-read directly this pass: the sentence quoted in the finding cell is exact, immediately followed by the mechanism reason ("Because `it.runIf(false)` never invokes the guarded test's body at all, no real fixture process was spawned").
